Document Index by Topic

OSA

  • 0710-mr-267.html -
    This mini-review discusses the conflicting effects of Intermittent Hypoxia (IH) on systemic hypertension, particularly its potential as a treatment due to its ability to lower blood pressure in individuals with hypertension. The review highlights research conducted primarily in the former Soviet Union, focusing on mechanisms such as enhanced sarcoplasmic reticular Ca2+ sequestration and increased antioxidant defenses that contribute to IH's effects on preventing endothelial dysfunction and high blood pressure in rats with various forms of experimental hypertension. The article also distinguishes Intermittent Hypoxia Treatment (IHT) from Obstructive Sleep Apnea, emphasizing IHT's potential benefits over traditional drug therapies due to its minimal side effects and possible stimulation of an organism's general resistance. However, the study concludes that further research is necessary to determine safe and effective protocols for IH application. The paper also provides references related to nitric oxide, intermittent hypoxia, and cardiovascular physiology.
  • 1-s2.0-S0022356525398290-main.html -
    This research explores the combined impact of fentanyl and xylazine on respiratory measures in mice, finding that the combination significantly reduces breathing rate more than fentanyl alone, with distinct effects on inspiration and expiration flow rates. Fentanyl decreases blood oxygen saturation and heart rate, while xylazine has less impact but exacerbates fentanyl's effects. The study suggests potential implications for anesthesia and sedation in mice, offering insights into mechanisms underlying xylazine's contribution to opioid overdoses in humans; however, these findings may not directly apply to humans due to the study being conducted on mice.
  • 1-s2.0-S0960982226002459-main.html -
    In a study published in Current Biology, Yamada et al. identified wPBN Oxtr neurons as key regulators of water consumption and discrimination in animals. These neurons facilitate water intake and are crucial for water discrimination, as inhibition of these neurons impairs the ability of mice to distinguish water from other liquids. The study offers insights into potential targets for therapies related to disorders involving fluid intake, such as anorexia nervosa and Prader-Willi syndrome. However, further research is needed to clarify the specific subpopulation responsible for water perception and the molecular basis of water perception by identifying associated peripheral sensory end organs.
  • Auricular nerve Stim and inflammation with COVID.html -
    The review article published in Rev Assoc Med Bras (2023) examines auricular vagus nerve stimulation (aVNS) as a potential novel treatment for managing inflammation related to COVID-19, suggesting it could modulate the immune response and reduce inflammatory markers, with more research required to confirm its efficacy and optimize usage.
  • Detecting sleep apnea.html -
    This study introduces an expert-level probabilistic breathing event detector (ABED), a deep learning model trained on over 5000 polysomnographies, to enhance sleep apnea phenotyping by providing accurate identification of various types of sleep-disordered breathing events and offering insights into their etiology. The model can outperform human experts in large-scale studies and shows potential benefits for increased diagnosis rates and more consistent scoring of apnea events. However, its robustness to low signal quality is limited for EEG and thoracic modalities.
  • ESS and stroke Capogrosso.html -
    The Nature Medicine study investigates the feasibility of cervical epidural spinal cord stimulation (SCS) for improving upper limb motor function in individuals with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis over a four-week period. Significant improvements were observed in strength, reduced spasticity, and increased Fugl-Meyer Assessment points. The study suggests that cervical SCS is safe, feasible, and effective for chronic post-stroke hemiparesis, but further research is needed to determine long-term effectiveness and safety, as well as the determinants of responsiveness to SCS, particularly spared sensory function.
  • Inspired CO2 in hypoxia improves endurance.html -
    This double-blind pilot study investigates the potential benefits of low-dose CO2 inhalation for enhancing oxygenation and exercise capacity at high altitudes, suggesting it may alleviate symptoms of Acute Mountain Sickness (AMS) and preserve functional performance. However, further research is required to confirm these findings and explore long-term effects on exercise performance and health outcomes.
  • Lei XI.ClinicalScience2026.html -
    The review article published in Clinical Science (2026) examines the impact of Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) on blood pressure regulation through disruption of circadian gene expression, focusing on the role of hypoxia-inducible factor 1α (HIF-1α). The research suggests that while conventional treatments like CPAP are effective for OSA, novel therapies targeting circadian clock genes could improve cardiovascular health, particularly in populations with disrupted sleep patterns or heightened salt sensitivity. Controlled moderate intermittent hypoxia is proposed to have potential benefits on the heart and vasculatures through modulation of specific gene expressions, but further research is needed for effective implementation of these novel therapies.
  • Mateika review hypercapnic AIH and LTF.html -
    The opinion paper published in The Journal of Physiology (2024) challenges the common belief that sustained hypercapnia is necessary for initiating plasticity during mild intermittent hypoxia exposure in humans, particularly in the context of Methylene blue-induced hypoxia with sustained carbon dioxide (MIHCO2). The paper proposes preliminary findings suggesting that MIHCO2 improves upper airway patency and has potential direct effects on cardiovascular outcomes, as shown by a significant decrease in blood pressure following repeated daily exposure to MIHCO2 in OSA patients treated nightly with CPAP. Future research will investigate the efficacy of different doses of MIHCO2, hypoxic sensitivity, and potential safety issues associated with exposure.
  • NIR and spinal O2 in SCI.html -
    The study "Clinical Application of Near-Infrared Blood Oxygen Monitoring in Cervical Spinal Cord Injury" investigates the use of near-infrared light-Oxygen saturation (NIR-SO2) technology to monitor spinal cord blood supply during surgery for cervical spinal cord injuries. The research, conducted on 37 patients, found an improvement in regional SO2 after laminectomy and durotomy, suggesting that NIR-SO2 is safe, feasible, and could potentially predict recovery and guide treatment choices for such patients.
  • Neuromodulation for the treatment of moderate to severe asthma a pilot first-in-human clinical study__.html -
    The July 2025 study published in the Journal of Asthma presents two pilot clinical trials investigating neuromodulation as a potential treatment for moderate to severe asthma. One trial used a non-invasive device to stimulate the vagus nerve, showing significant improvements in pulmonary function tests and asthma symptom scores after six weeks for 10 participants. The other study involved SCS implantation in 11 patients with chronic pain, resulting in complete elimination of asthma symptoms within days, improved asthma control, reduced medication usage, and enhanced quality of life. Future research will focus on larger controlled clinical studies to further establish the safety and efficacy of these neuromodulation techniques as treatment options for asthma patients.
  • SOS R Wilson.html -
    The study by Barioni et al., published in Science Advances, identifies a novel oxygen sensing mechanism in the spinal cord's sympathetic preganglionic neurons (SSPNs), which contributes to cardiorespiratory responses during hypoxia. This mechanism involves nitric oxide synthase 1 (NOS1) and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH) oxidase (NOX2). The research has potential clinical implications for various respiratory and sympathetic conditions, such as obesity, atherosclerosis, diabetes, asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, sleep apnea, acute mountain sickness, subdued carotid body activity, intermittent hypoxia-induced cardiorespiratory plasticity, and severe asphyxia-like conditions.
  • Semiglutide and CoB.html -
    The scientific paper published in Acta Physiologica (2026) explores the potential for Glucagon-Like Peptide-1 Receptor Agonists (GLP1RAs), traditionally used for Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (TD2M) and obesity, to address respiratory disorders like Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA). The study finds that Liraglutide and Tirzepatide, including the dual receiver long-acting agonist Tirzepatide which was recently approved by the FDA for OSA in overweight individuals, reduce AHI in patients with obesity and moderate/severe OSA. The research suggests that GLP1RAs may have a promising role in treating OSA, independent of weight loss, and ongoing clinical trials for triple-hormone receptor agonist retatrutide offer hope for future treatments in this area.
  • The Journal of Physiology - 2026 - Gold - Amino acid modulation of the carotid body selectively modulates peripheral.html -
    This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.
  • The_hypoxic_ventilatory_response_and_arousal_burde.html -
    In a 2025 study, researchers led by Jason H. Mateika found that hypoxic ventilatory response (HVR) and arousal burden are significant predictors of progressive augmentation (PA) and ventilatory long-term facilitation (VLTF) in individuals with obstructive sleep apnea (OSA). Arousal burden might be a more accurate predictor of the magnitude of PA or VLTF than hypoxic burden. The study was funded by several institutions, including the United States Department of Veterans Affairs and the American Academy of Sleep Medicine Foundation.
  • Vocal biomarker for sleep apnea.html -
    The study led by Shiva Akbari et al. investigates the use of voice biomarkers to predict Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) subtypes during wakefulness, identifying four distinct patterns with high accuracy. This novel approach could facilitate early diagnosis and personalized treatment for OSA patients based on speech analysis. The researchers emphasize the need for further validation in larger populations and diverse demographics.
  • aging of the pulmonary system.html -
    This scientific paper, written by the International Consortium to Classify Ageing-Related Pathologies (ICCARP) respiratory working group, explores genetic, epigenetic, and inflammatory factors contributing to age-related changes in lung function. Key findings suggest chronic low-grade inflammation plays a significant role in these changes, while senescent cells, telomere shortening, and altered secretomes also contribute. The paper proposes classifying pulmonary fibrosis driven by senescence as an ageing-related pathology and advocates for a multiprofessional approach to manage lung aging due to its complex nature involving both physiological and psychological aspects. However, the research relies heavily on inference from currently classified diseases and has a paucity of human data, highlighting the need for further study of human tissue samples across various ages to better understand lung ageing, particularly regarding the mucociliary system.
  • brainstem astrocytes and respiratory behavior.html -
    This study under peer review at Nature Communications investigates the role of specific astrocytes in the ventral respiratory column (VRC) of mice, revealing they modulate sigh generation and arousal transitions, are important for the ventilatory and arousal response to hypoxia, and may have implications for understanding sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS). The findings suggest that these medullary astrocytes play a crucial role in activating catecholaminergic neurons and increasing calcium transients prior to arousal with sighs.
  • drugs for OSA review.html -
    The scientific paper advocates for a personalized approach to treating Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA), focusing on six potential pharmacological strategies including enhancing upper airway dilator muscle activity, stabilizing breathing control, blocking cardiovascular consequences, addressing symptoms, promoting CPAP adherence, and facilitating weight loss. Promising results have been shown for a drug combination of atomoxetine and oxybutynin, GLP-1 receptor agonists like tirzepatide, and future pharmacological strategies include orforglipron and retatrutide. The authors suggest that a combination of therapies targeting mechanical airway obstruction, underlying obesity, and cardiovascular risk factors will optimize clinical outcomes for OSA patients, with CPAP remaining the standard of care for OSA evaluation and treatment by sleep medicine specialists.
  • e0550262026.full.html -
    This scientific paper by Arakawa et al. investigates the role of hypothalamic orexin (ORX) and glutamatergic neurons in counteracting fentanyl-induced respiratory depression (FIRD) in mice, focusing on the lateral hypothalamus (LH) and paraventricular nucleus (PVN). The study found that LH-ORX/glutamatergic projections to the medulla drive respiratory activity, reducing FIRD. Activation of PVN glutamatergic neurons had minimal effects on respiratory parameters. The research suggests potential therapeutic targets in FIRD, particularly LH glutamatergic neurons projecting to the preBötC stimulating breathing via ORX signaling; however, further studies are needed to determine the role of PVN excitatory neurons in attenuating FIRD.
  • jamaneurology_neilson_2025_oi_250080_1763581277.53113.html -
    The scientific paper 'Obstructive Sleep Apnea, Positive Airway Pressure, and Implications of Early Treatment in Parkinson Disease' suggests that Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) is a modifiable midlife risk factor for developing Parkinson's disease (PD), especially among women. The study found a higher incidence of PD among veterans with OSA compared to those without, and early treatment with CPAP significantly reduced the number of PD cases and associated risks such as falls, fractures, and mortality. However, limitations include an inability to assess CPAP adherence beyond administrative codes and a need for further prospective studies to confirm and extend these findings.
  • lung as a sensory organ NIH.html -
    The scientific paper discusses a workshop on 'Lung Sensing', a sensory interface beyond gas exchange in the lungs. The research focuses on understanding various types of sensory cells involved, their roles in lung diseases like COPD, asthma, and small-cell lung cancer, and potential future directions for studying lung sensing to uncover novel therapeutic targets and improve disease management.
  • monitoring_spinal_cord_tissue_oxygen_in_patients.32.html -
    The study published in Critical Care Medicine investigates the use of an intraspinal pressure catheter and tissue oxygen monitor to assess cord tissue oxygen levels at the site of spinal cord injury in 26 acute spinal cord injury patients. Key findings include associations between tissue oxygen levels and Fio2, Pao2, intraspinal pressure, spinal cord perfusion pressure, sedation level, glucose, lactate/pyruvate ratio, fever, blood transfusions, cerebrospinal fluid drainage, and motor-complete outcomes. Monitoring tissue oxygen could potentially provide valuable information for managing patients with acute spinal cord injuries, but further research is needed to confirm these findings and determine clinical utility. Limitations include a small sample size and complications such as cerebrospinal fluid leaks.
  • nn1166.html -
    In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.
  • opioid-induced-respiratory-depression peripheral effects 2026.html -
    The scientific paper "Pulmonary and carotid body afferents in Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression (OIRD)" investigates the effects of opioids on various bodily systems, particularly the respiratory system, through a review of previous studies and experiments on rodent models. Key findings reveal that morphine and enkephalins activate vagal pulmonary C-fiber afferents, causing apnea and increased laryngeal nerve activity, while opioids also stimulate carotid body activity via κ-opioid receptors (k -ORs), involving calcium signaling in glomus cells. The research provides insights into potential new therapeutic strategies for managing OIRD.
  • peng-et-al-2026-intracellular-calcium-release-activates-carotid-body-afferents-by-fentanyl.html -
    The research reveals that fentanyl activates carotid body afferents through a KOR-Gaq-PLC-IP3R-Ca2+ signaling pathway, which increases intracellular calcium levels in glomus cells and counteracts opioid-induced respiratory depression. This newly discovered mechanism may help reduce opioid-related respiratory complications and enhance opioid safety.
  • s41593-026-02279-z.html -
    The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow. The findings suggest a mechanical link between the brain and the abdominal compartment, with implications for understanding pathologies such as intracranial hypertension, meningioma, Chiari malformations, and conditions like obesity that elevate intra-abdominal pressure.
  • sieck-fogarty-2025-diaphragm-muscle-a-pump-that-can-not-fail.html -
    In a 2025 review article published by the American Physiological Society, researchers emphasize the diaphragm muscle's crucial role as a primary pump in mammals, comparable to the heart, beyond breathing functions like suckling at birth and airway defense. The paper discusses various conditions affecting the diaphragm muscle, such as myasthenia gravis, diaphragm and intercostal muscle pump failures, and explores the importance of understanding the detailed neural control of the diaphragm muscle for respiratory and non-respiratory functions.

TBI_SCI

  • 1-s2.0-S0092867426006380-main.html -
    The Cell-published study by Belliveau et al. confirms TMEM154/Galvanin as a cellular antenna that senses electrical gradients, facilitating cell migration towards the cathode. They found that Galvanin is essential for electrotaxis in human granulocytes and can transform non-electrotactic cell types into electrotactic ones, suggesting an electrophoretic process where Galvanin redistributes towards the anodal pole of the cell in electrical fields.
  • 1-s2.0-S0896627325008037-main.html -
    The study by Zhou et al. discovers that a pro-inflammatory microglial subtype (C5aR1+) amplifies neuroinflammation, promotes harmful astrocyte polarization, and enhances neutrophil recruitment after traumatic brain injury (TBI) or intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH), exacerbating cerebral edema and neurological deficits. Pharmacological inhibition of C5aR1 reduces edema in preclinical models, suggesting its potential as a therapeutic target for TBI and ICH. The FDA-approved drug avacopan, a C5aR1 antagonist, shows promise in mitigating these harmful effects by suppressing pro-inflammatory cytokine expression, restoring microglial morphology, inhibiting AKT and ERK phosphorylation, reducing astrocyte polarization, decreasing infiltrated neutrophils, alleviating cerebral edema, and accelerating functional recovery after TBI and ICH. Further research is needed to confirm these findings and explore the full therapeutic potential of avacopan in these conditions.
  • 1-s2.0-S2211124726003657-main.html -
    The study "Vip+ vagal neurons control allergen-induced responses" found that Vip+ vagal sensory neurons play a crucial role in asthma pathogenesis by mediating airway hyperreactivity and type 2 inflammation through the neurotrophin-NGFR signaling axis. These neurons, which are distinct from Tac1+ neurons, project extensively to the nTS region of the brainstem, revealing a neuroimmune mechanism along the body-brain axis contributing to asthma. The study highlights the functional importance of NGFR signaling in Vip+-neuron-mediated allergen responses, but further studies are needed to determine the role of VIP neuropeptide and its primary functional source in driving allergen responses.
  • Breathwork post-concussion.html -
    The study "Slow Breathing Interventions for Athletes with Sport-Related Concussion: A Randomized Controlled Trial" finds that Resonance Breathing (RB) and Progressive Relaxation Breathing (PRB) reduce symptoms and improve cognitive functioning in athletes recovering from concussions by boosting prefrontal theta activity, enhancing attentional regulation, and potentially improving autonomic signaling coordination. However, the study's limitations include a small sample size, gender bias, exploratory correlation analyses, and lack of a sham control condition, suggesting that further research is needed to validate these findings as beneficial adjunctive approaches for concussion rehabilitation.
  • CNS Neuroscience Therapeutics - 2026 - Jing - The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates.html -
    This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
  • Federal funding of SCI research.html -
    This study evaluates federal funding for spinal cord injury research from 2008-2025 across three primary agencies - National Institutes of Health, Department of Defense's Congressionally Directed Medical Research Programs, and the Department of Veterans Affairs. The researchers found that NIH focuses on basic and early-stage translational research in SCI pathology, regeneration, and motor functional recovery. In contrast, CDMRP funds applied and clinical research primarily in pain, bladder function, and neuromodulatory device development, while the VA invests in rehabilitation studies aimed at musculoskeletal and functional health outcomes. Critical funding gaps were identified in areas like bowel/gastrointestinal health, cardiovascular function, and mental health. The study warns of a 50% decline in federal SCI research funding due to proposed NIH budget reductions, highlighting the need for coordinated, data-driven strategies and the development of a "living dashboard" for enhanced transparency and strategic investment in SCI research.
  • GABAA receptor gating of proprioceptive afferent transmission--serotonin.html -
    The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.
  • In vivo multiplex imaging of dynamic neurochemical networks.html -
    The study introduces HaloDA1.0, a novel far-red dopamine sensor developed by combining the cpHaloTag-chemical dye approach with the G protein-coupled receptor activation-based strategy. It offers high sensitivity for dopamine, sub-second response kinetics, and an extensive spectral range, enabling multi-color imaging in various neural systems. Key findings include successful detection of dopamine release following Ventral Tegmental Area (VTA) stimulation, compatibility for dual-color recordings during optogenetic stimulation in the central nucleus of the amygdala (CeA), and simultaneous monitoring of dopamine, acetylcholine (ACh), and cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) dynamics in the mouse Nucleus Accumbens (NAc). The chemigenetic strategy offers potential for developing a wide range of far-red neuromodulator sensors based on other GPCRs.
  • SCI and Inflammation Weaver.html -
    This study by Fleming et al. (2006) investigates the cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury, focusing on neutrophils, monocytes/macrophages, lymphocytes, oxidative enzymes, and proteolytic enzymes. The research found that neutrophils dominate immediately after injury but decrease over time, while macrophages increase. Activated microglia express gp91phox and contribute to both neurological recovery and tissue damage. Lymphocytes, particularly CD8+ T-cells, are present among macrophages, with their role in secondary degenerative processes and wound healing unclear. The study highlights the need for limiting neutrophil entry as a potential neuroprotective therapy.
  • TMS test and retest.html -
    The study investigates the test-retest reliability of neuroexcitability measures using threshold-tracking methods in both healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury (SCI). It finds excellent relative reliability for TMS measures in both groups, high test-retest reliability for NET using the TROND protocol across all age groups, and the T-T RMT test being more efficient. Most parameters showed good to excellent reliability over time, but hyperpolarization slope may have lower reliability. The study concludes that threshold-tracking-based techniques are reliable and suitable for clinical research in populations with spasticity like those with stroke or SCI.
  • elife-107480-v1.html -
    The study by Shevtsova et al., published in eLife, investigates the recovery of locomotor function following thoracic spinal cord injuries (hemisection or contusion) in rats using computational models. Key findings indicate that post-injury gait expression is significantly impacted by the drive to sublesional lumbar rhythm generators and lumbar commissural interneuron connectivity, with a critical balance between V0 and V3 connectivity for maintaining gait expression. The research offers insights into potential therapeutic targets for spinal cord injuries by restoring sublesional lumbar rhythm-generating circuits and commissural function tailored to injury symmetry.
  • kumar-et-al-2026-a-motor-thalamic-site-in-humans-that-suppresses-involuntary-breathing-without-awareness.html -
    The study led by Sukhbinder Kumar et al. identifies a specific region within the human motor thalamus (VLA and VA nuclei) that, when stimulated, induces central apnea without sensory or motor effects, awareness, or impact on volitional breathing or speech. This discovery offers insights into the neural mechanisms of respiratory control and potential implications for understanding and treating various respiratory disorders such as sleep apnea, SUDEP (Sudden Unexpected Death in Epilepsy), and SIDS.
  • s41386-026-02457-4.html -
    This study investigates the role of GABAergic neurons in the ventrolateral periaqueductal gray (vlPAG) during fentanyl withdrawal and self-administration in mice, revealing an increased activity and enhanced inhibitory synaptic transmission within this region during fentanyl use and withdrawal. Chemogenetic inhibition of these neurons alleviated withdrawal-induced hyperalgesia in male mice, while no comparable effect was observed in female mice. The findings suggest that targeting inhibitory signaling within the vlPAG could potentially alleviate withdrawal-associated hyperalgesia and limit fentanyl use.
  • s41467-026-73828-0_reference.html -
    The research "Human Forebrain Neural Synchronization and Entrainment to Breathing During Wakefulness, Sleep, and External Mechanical Ventilation" found that the human forebrain synchronizes with breathing rhythms during various states, including wakefulness, sleep, and mechanical ventilation. The study discovered multiple afferent pathways for this synchronization, with possible unique changes in humans compared to rodents. Key brain regions like the insula, somatosensory cortex, anterior cingulate cortex, amygdala, and hippocampus showed synchronization during wakefulness, and the amygdala and hippocampus continued this synchronization during sleep. The findings could have implications for understanding sleep apnea, epilepsy, and other neurological disorders.
  • science.adw1803.html -
    The study led by Longzhong Jia et al. investigates a novel radial glial cell population (SVZ RGC) in the human medial ganglionic eminence (hMGE), which plays a crucial role in producing GABAergic inhibitory neurons and glial cells during human brain development. The findings offer insights into unique mechanisms of human brain development, potentially contributing to understanding neurological disorders like autism spectrum disorder.

attention

  • Auricular nerve Stim and inflammation with COVID.html -
    The review article published in Rev Assoc Med Bras (2023) examines auricular vagus nerve stimulation (aVNS) as a potential novel treatment for managing inflammation related to COVID-19, suggesting it could modulate the immune response and reduce inflammatory markers, with more research required to confirm its efficacy and optimize usage.
  • Neuroscience of Cancer.html -
    The scientific paper investigates complex interactions between the nervous system, cancer cells, and immune cells in various types of cancers, such as glioma, gastrointestinal, oral squamous cell carcinomas, and pancreatic cancer. It reveals that neuronal activity promotes cancer progression, co-opted by cancer cells to enhance metastasis potential. Key findings suggest new treatment strategies like preoperative β-blockade, serotonin attenuation, and vagus nerve stimulation for cancer management.

brainstem

  • 1-s2.0-S016622362600010X-main.html -
    This scientific paper explores the complex regulation of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF), a protein essential for brain development, learning, and memory, focusing on various transcription factors and four enhancer regions within the BDNF gene. The research also emphasizes post-transcriptional regulation of BDNF, underscoring the roles of distinct 5' and 3' UTRs in controlling subcellular localization and translation. The study highlights the need for further investigation into the regulatory landscape, non-neural tissue expression, and physiological significance of 3' UTRs in regulating cell type-specific BDNF expression to better understand its intricate regulatory processes and implications for brain health and disease.
  • 1-s2.0-S0896627325009353-main.html -
    The scientific paper by Fenstermacher et al. investigates the roles of spinal-projecting serotonergic (5-HT) neurons in locomotor behavior using optogenetic techniques on mice. Activation of these neurons increases the speed and duration of ongoing locomotion, with key findings showing an increase in individual run bouts' length, fewer overall run bouts, and a shift towards faster speeds during locomotion. The study suggests that serotonergic neurons can potentiate motor output over extended time scales, enhancing locomotor output and modulating the temporal dynamics of motor circuits. The research has potential implications for understanding the function of serotonin in motor behavior and developing innovative approaches to control locomotion in neuroprosthetics.
  • 1-s2.0-S0960982226002459-main.html -
    In a study published in Current Biology, Yamada et al. identified wPBN Oxtr neurons as key regulators of water consumption and discrimination in animals. These neurons facilitate water intake and are crucial for water discrimination, as inhibition of these neurons impairs the ability of mice to distinguish water from other liquids. The study offers insights into potential targets for therapies related to disorders involving fluid intake, such as anorexia nervosa and Prader-Willi syndrome. However, further research is needed to clarify the specific subpopulation responsible for water perception and the molecular basis of water perception by identifying associated peripheral sensory end organs.
  • 1-s2.0-S1081120626001560-main.html -
    The 2026 review "Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease" published by the American College of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology explores the neurology behind both acute and chronic cough, focusing on vagal sensory neurons and brainstem circuits. The study suggests that chronic cough is associated with peripheral sensory nerve fiber sensitivity and central neural mechanisms amplification, leading to excessive coughing in response to low levels of thermal, mechanical, or chemical exposure. Brain imaging studies suggest chronic cough may be a disorder of reduced inhibitory control, and therapies restoring sensorimotor balance could potentially control excessive cough. The review highlights the clinical significance of chronic cough and significant therapeutic advances in recent years for managing it, though uniformity in responsivity to trialed drugs indicates the need for therapies directed at specific endotypic mechanisms or responsive treatable traits for effective cough management. Funding was provided by the National Health and Medical Research Council of Australia, with some authors receiving grants and consulting fees from various pharmaceutical companies.
  • 1-s2.0-S1569904826000017-main.html -
    The study reveals that dexmedetomidine, a sedative and analgesic drug, causes biphasic temporal suppression of swallowing and respiration, particularly under hypoxic conditions, during superior laryngeal nerve stimulation in rats. The research also suggests the SLN's role in baroreflex and minimal potentiation of GABAergic pathways contributing to bradycardia in animals during continuous dexmedetomidine infusion.
  • 1-s2.0-S2211124726003657-main.html -

  • Ado and ATP Link PCO2 to CSE via pH.html -
    This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.
  • BDNF and platelets in exercise and hypoxia.html -
    This study published in 'The Journal of Physiology' investigates the role of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF) and Platelet Factor 4 (PF4) during exercise at sea level and high altitude, revealing that maximal exercise doubles free BDNF concentration, with platelets playing a role in liberating BDNF and potentially contributing to the neuroprotective effects of exercise. At high altitude, hypoxia promotes net cerebral release of BDNF, and there is a correlation between platelet loss during exercise and PF4 release into venous circulation.
  • Breathwork post-concussion.html -
    The study "Slow Breathing Interventions for Athletes with Sport-Related Concussion: A Randomized Controlled Trial" finds that Resonance Breathing (RB) and Progressive Relaxation Breathing (PRB) reduce symptoms and improve cognitive functioning in athletes recovering from concussions by boosting prefrontal theta activity, enhancing attentional regulation, and potentially improving autonomic signaling coordination. However, the study's limitations include a small sample size, gender bias, exploratory correlation analyses, and lack of a sham control condition, suggesting that further research is needed to validate these findings as beneficial adjunctive approaches for concussion rehabilitation.
  • CNS Neuroscience Therapeutics - 2026 - Jing - The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates.html -
    This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
  • CNS lactate and neuroplasticity - 2026.html -
    The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.
  • GABAA receptor gating of proprioceptive afferent transmission--serotonin.html -
    The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.
  • Gaba hub neurons organize the brain.html -
    The scientific paper investigates the significant role of high connectivity GABAergic interneurons, or hub neurons, in regulating network dynamics during hippocampus development, particularly their impact on Giant Depolarizing Potentials (GDPs). Key findings show that a third of targeted hub neurons significantly affect network dynamics compared to minimal impact from low connectivity neurons. Hub neurons are predominantly found in specific layers like the stratum oriens and lucidum, and activation of these neurons before synchrony contributes to network synchronization in the developing CA3 region. The study also highlights differences between hub and low connectivity interneurons in terms of electrophysiological properties and references notable authors' works to provide insights into physiological and pathological network oscillations.
  • Glymphatic Scientific American.html -
    The scientific paper investigates the glymphatic system, a nocturnal waste removal mechanism in the brain essential for clearing toxic proteins linked to Alzheimer's disease, focusing on the role of norepinephrine and anesthetics' impact on its functionality. Key findings reveal that certain anesthetics can either suppress or enhance glymphatic function, with impairments linked to various conditions such as chronic pain, psychiatric disorders, and neurodegenerative diseases. The research suggests potential for non-invasive diagnostic tools like a "glymphogram" and treatments for slowing or preventing neurodegenerative diseases.
  • Inhibition of HcVR by Ado in RTN.html -
    The scientific paper explores how adenosine decreases reversible and dose-dependent respiratory activity in rats during hypercapnia, potentially through A1 receptor binding on brainstem neurons. However, limitations necessitate further research to fully understand adenosine's role in the RTN and its influence on respiratory function, with funding provided by various organizations.
  • NE and State of Pre-Bo network.html -
    This preprint study investigates the effects of Norepinephrine (NE) and Nitric Oxide (NO) on the preBotanic Complex (preBötC), a critical brainstem region for respiratory rhythm generation, in rodents. The research finds that NE increases action potential frequency while decreasing the average action potential number during network bursts, and NO attenuates synchrony by activating soluble guanylyl cyclase and cGMP-dependent protein kinase (PKG). Combined application of NA and NO leads to complex interactions with overall effects dependent on their relative concentrations. The study suggests potential roles for NE and NO modulation in abnormal respiratory patterns associated with chronic hypoxia, offering insights for new therapeutic strategies for treating hypoxia-related respiratory disorders. However, further peer review is necessary as this is a preprint.
  • Neuroscience of Cancer.html -
    The scientific paper investigates complex interactions between the nervous system, cancer cells, and immune cells in various types of cancers, such as glioma, gastrointestinal, oral squamous cell carcinomas, and pancreatic cancer. It reveals that neuronal activity promotes cancer progression, co-opted by cancer cells to enhance metastasis potential. Key findings suggest new treatment strategies like preoperative β-blockade, serotonin attenuation, and vagus nerve stimulation for cancer management.
  • Semiglutide and CoB.html -
    The scientific paper published in Acta Physiologica (2026) explores the potential for Glucagon-Like Peptide-1 Receptor Agonists (GLP1RAs), traditionally used for Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (TD2M) and obesity, to address respiratory disorders like Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA). The study finds that Liraglutide and Tirzepatide, including the dual receiver long-acting agonist Tirzepatide which was recently approved by the FDA for OSA in overweight individuals, reduce AHI in patients with obesity and moderate/severe OSA. The research suggests that GLP1RAs may have a promising role in treating OSA, independent of weight loss, and ongoing clinical trials for triple-hormone receptor agonist retatrutide offer hope for future treatments in this area.
  • alpha syneuclin and aquaporin 4.html -
    This research paper suggests that mislocalization of AQP4, a water channel protein in the perivascular region, impairs glymphatic function, which is responsible for clearing alpha-synuclein from brain tissue. This impairment promotes the aggregation of alpha-synuclein and potentially contributes to the development and propagation of synucleinopathies like Lewy Body Dementia and Parkinson's disease. The findings are based on post-mortem human brain tissue analysis and experiments using transgenic mice, and suggest that glymphatic impairment may contribute to the development and progression of synucleinopathies.
  • annurev-neuro-082625-012115.html -
    This review paper published in Annual Review of Neuroscience (2026) emphasizes the role of the lower brainstem, specifically key regions such as the Nucleus of the Solitary Tract, Parabrachial Nucleus, Medulla, Locus Coeruleus, and others, in regulating sleep-wake cycles, arousal, and motor output. The lower brainstem functions as an integrated network linking sleep-wake regulation to the body's homeostatic demands, serving as a master coordinator for global brain states and peripheral bodily functions, which has implications for developing treatments for sleep disorders.
  • astrocytes and brain gases.html -
    Astrocytes play a critical role in brain oxygen sensing, cerebral perfusion, and cognition, with a higher sensitivity to changes in oxygen availability compared to glomus cells. They respond to hypoxia by releasing nitric oxide, regulating sympathetic drive, and cardiac output to maintain brain oxygenation. However, during ischemia, astrocytes can both protect neurons and exacerbate brain damage due to their involvement in neurotransmitter release, calcium signaling, and gap junction communication. Future research should explore therapeutic interventions targeting astrocytic functions for improving neurological disorders related to oxygen homeostasis, cognitive impairment, and dementia.
  • brainstem astrocytes and respiratory behavior.html -
    This study under peer review at Nature Communications investigates the role of specific astrocytes in the ventral respiratory column (VRC) of mice, revealing they modulate sigh generation and arousal transitions, are important for the ventilatory and arousal response to hypoxia, and may have implications for understanding sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS). The findings suggest that these medullary astrocytes play a crucial role in activating catecholaminergic neurons and increasing calcium transients prior to arousal with sighs.
  • dempsey-mccrimmon-2026-vco2-exercise hyperpnea.html -
    The scientific paper by Jerome A. Dempsey and Donald R. McCrimmon, published in the John Rankin Laboratory of Pulmonary Medicine, explores the significant role of carbon dioxide production (V̇CO2) in regulating arterial carbon dioxide pressure (PaCO2), challenging the traditional view that CO2-sensitive chemoreceptors are crucial for maintaining normal PaCO2 homeostasis. The study's findings suggest adjustments in ventilation to maintain near-homeostatic PaCO2 levels during exercise, changes in dead space ventilation (Vd:VT), or age without modifications in CO2 chemoreceptor responsiveness. Potential mechanisms for this link include adaptive control/associative learning and three sites for mediation: pulmonary vasculature and airways, muscle afferents sensing blood flow as a metabolic surrogate, and the Reticular Formation of the brainstem (RTN) integration. The paper encourages further research to advance our understanding of exercise hyperpnea and error-free regulation of ventilation to CO2 production, particularly for populations with respiratory disorders like COPD.
  • e2199252026.full.html -
    The study conducted by researchers from the University of Florida and University of Michigan reveals that locus ceruleus (LC) neurons control breathing through noradrenaline and glutamate release, exerting flexible, opposing effects on different timescales in the key respiratory brainstem nucleus. The research provides evidence for vesicular glutamate transporter 2 (vGluT2) involvement in glutamate release from LC neurons to the kernicterus nucleus and suggests implications for understanding respiratory disorders where LC function is compromised.
  • glymphatic drainage review.html -
    The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. Key findings reveal primary CSF influx along periarterial spaces, solute clearance through multiple routes, and functional influence by vascular pulsation, neural activity, sleep, and immunity. The study highlights the role of immune cells at brain-border niches in monitoring solute efflux, linking brain clearance to neuroimmune interactions. The research aims to uncover new therapeutic avenues for various disorders such as autoimmune, neurodegenerative, neoplastic, and psychiatric diseases.
  • guerrier-et-al-2015-robust-network-oscillations-during-mammalian-respiratory-rhythm-generation-driven-by-synaptic-1.html -
    The research by Guerrier et al. presents a computational model that replicates the eupneic respiratory rhythm observed experimentally within the preBötzinger complex (preBötC) using a randomly connected neuronal network with short-term synaptic facilitation and depression. The study validates its findings against various experimental conditions and emphasizes the significance of understanding synaptic vesicle cycles, calibrated brainstem slices, and modeling spontaneous activity in various cortical areas for further advancements in neuroscience.
  • kumar-et-al-2026-a-motor-thalamic-site-in-humans-that-suppresses-involuntary-breathing-without-awareness.html -
    The study led by Sukhbinder Kumar et al. identifies a specific region within the human motor thalamus (VLA and VA nuclei) that, when stimulated, induces central apnea without sensory or motor effects, awareness, or impact on volitional breathing or speech. This discovery offers insights into the neural mechanisms of respiratory control and potential implications for understanding and treating various respiratory disorders such as sleep apnea, SUDEP (Sudden Unexpected Death in Epilepsy), and SIDS.

    The scientific paper discusses a workshop on 'Lung Sensing', a sensory interface beyond gas exchange in the lungs.   • macedo-et-al-2026-respiratory-control-dysfunction-and-vulnerability-in-parkinson-s-disease.html -
    The study "Respiratory Control in Parkinson's Disease" by Tainá O. Macedo et al., funded by research grants, investigates how Parkinson's disease affects central neural circuits like the RTN and preBötC, causing oxidative stress, neuroinflammation, mitochondrial dysfunction, and neuronal vulnerability. Peripherally, PD impacts sleep architecture, altering rapid eye movement (REM) and non-REM sleep stages through glutamatergic neurons in the PPN. Respiratory dysfunction, a common but underdiagnosed feature of PD, is observed in decreased inspiratory drive, increased expiratory flow limitation, and changes in response to hypercapnia and hypoxia. Pharmacological interventions have been explored for restoring respiratory function, while the authors recommend standardizing methods for assessing respiratory function in clinical settings and designing clinical trials targeting respiratory outcomes. The study provides a list of references related to sleep, arousal systems, cardiorespiratory regulation, Parkinson's disease, and central chemoreceptors.
  • moreira-takakura-2026-urethane-and-vagotomy-reshape-cardiorespiratory-homeostasis-and-rtn-chemoreceptor-function.html -
    The research investigates how urethane anesthesia and vagotomy alter cardiorespiratory homeostasis and the function of retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN) chemoreceptors in rats, revealing that these interventions reshape central chemoreceptor response by decreasing sensitivity to hypercapnia and hypoxia, emphasizing the importance of vagal feedback in central respiratory control.
  • music and the brain.html -
    The study "Finding the Groove in Neural Space" investigates how the human brain processes rhythmic patterns by examining neural signatures during rhythmic tapping tasks in two participants with spinal cord injuries. Findings suggest that motor cortex encodes position, velocity, and tempo-dependent differences simultaneously and a continuous tempo axis exists in neural space. The research reveals a continuum of kinematic strategies during rhythmic tapping tasks, indicating shifts from reactive to anticipatory timing mechanisms at lower and higher tempos respectively. The study has implications for developing more effective brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) and understanding human brain function.
  • nihms169805.html -
    The paper reviews recent advancements in understanding the neural mechanisms that generate rhythmic breathing patterns (rhythmicity), adaptive changes in breathing patterns (plasticity), and the role of chemoreceptors in regulating respiratory drive based on blood gas changes (chemosensitivity). Key findings include identifying the preBötzinger Complex as a key site for generating the respiratory rhythm, discussing serotonin-dependent long-term facilitation during intermittent hypoxia, and highlighting neurons with chemosensitivity spread throughout the brainstem. These studies could lead to more effective therapeutic strategies for various respiratory control disorders like Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), sleep-disordered breathing or sleep apnea, Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome, and respiratory insufficiency in spinally injured patients.
  • nn1166.html -
    In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.
  • plastic astrocyte networks.html -
    This study investigates the role of connexin43 (Cx43) gap junctions in astrocyte networks within the mouse brain, revealing their significance in maintaining network connectivity, brain functioning, and potential implications for neurodegenerative diseases. The research employs various methods including SDS-PAGE gel electrophoresis, Western blotting, qPCR, and microscopy techniques to visualize astrocyte networks and study the effects of Cx43 deletion in astrocytes. Key findings suggest that astrocyte structural plasticity plays a critical role in regulating neural circuit function and behavior, offering potential insights for new treatments in neurodegenerative diseases.
  • reilly-et-al-2026-a-projection-atlas-of-excitatory-and-inhibitory-inputs-to-the-prebötzinger-complex-substrates-for.html -
    The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). Inhibitory projections were predominantly ipsilateral to the injection site in the caudal medulla, while excitatory projections were found in the contralateral cVRG. This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.
  • s00424-025-03147-8.html -
    The research "Investigating the Impact of Exercise on Cardiovascular Function in Elderly Individuals" published in Pflügers Archiv - European Journal of Physiology (2026) demonstrates that regular exercise improves cardiovascular health in elderly populations, while a separate study explores the role of substance P and GR82334 on respiratory muscle activity and neural minute volume in rodents. The latter study suggests that substance P signaling plays a critical role in modulating respiratory output under both basal and hypercapnic conditions through NK1 receptors in tachykinin-producing neurons in the ventral pFRG (posterior medullary tegmentum, also known as the rostral ventrolateral medulla).
  • s41467-026-73828-0_reference.html -
    The research "Human Forebrain Neural Synchronization and Entrainment to Breathing During Wakefulness, Sleep, and External Mechanical Ventilation" found that the human forebrain synchronizes with breathing rhythms during various states, including wakefulness, sleep, and mechanical ventilation. The study discovered multiple afferent pathways for this synchronization, with possible unique changes in humans compared to rodents. Key brain regions like the insula, somatosensory cortex, anterior cingulate cortex, amygdala, and hippocampus showed synchronization during wakefulness, and the amygdala and hippocampus continued this synchronization during sleep. The findings could have implications for understanding sleep apnea, epilepsy, and other neurological disorders.
  • s41586-025-09755-9.html -
    This research investigates the role of adenosine in ketamine's antidepressant effects, revealing that ketamine increases adenosine levels in the mouse brain, particularly in the prefrontal cortex and hippocampus. The study suggests DCK (2C-DCK) and 3′-Cl-ketamine as promising new antidepressants due to their stronger and longer-lasting adenosine surges than ketamine. The research also explores the role of adenosine signaling in Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT) and intermittent hypoxia, and uses genetically engineered sensors to investigate ATP/ADP ratios in neural activity and behavior, potentially providing insights into various neurological and psychiatric disorders.
  • s41593-026-02279-z.html -
    The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow. The findings suggest a mechanical link between the brain and the abdominal compartment, with implications for understanding pathologies such as intracranial hypertension, meningioma, Chiari malformations, and conditions like obesity that elevate intra-abdominal pressure.
  • s41598-017-09418-4.html -
    This 2017 Nature Scientific Reports study investigates the unique characteristics of Dbx1 and non-Dbx1 neurons within the preBötC, a respiratory control center in the brain, through gene expression analysis. Key findings include Dbx1 neurons being primary excitatory, glutamatergic neurons, with some exhibiting chloride-mediated synaptic inhibition, and differences in the expression of transcription factors and Synaptotagmin-10 between these neuron types. The study provides a foundation for further research into the neural generation and control of breathing.

    The study led by Longzhong Jia et al. investigates a novel radial glial cell population (SVZ RGC) in the human medial ganglionic eminence (hMGE), which plays a crucial role in producing GABAergic inhibitory neurons and glial cells during human brain development. The findings offer insights into unique mechanisms of human brain development, potentially contributing to understanding neurological disorders like autism spectrum disorder.
  • sieck-fogarty-2025-diaphragm-muscle-a-pump-that-can-not-fail.html -
    In a 2025 review article published by the American Physiological Society, researchers emphasize the diaphragm muscle's crucial role as a primary pump in mammals, comparable to the heart, beyond breathing functions like suckling at birth and airway defense. The paper discusses various conditions affecting the diaphragm muscle, such as myasthenia gravis, diaphragm and intercostal muscle pump failures, and explores the importance of understanding the detailed neural control of the diaphragm muscle for respiratory and non-respiratory functions.

  • Adenosine and Sleep Review .html -
    The review article "Adenosine, caffeine, and sleep–wake regulation: state of the science and perspectives" investigates the effects of adenosine and caffeine on sleep regulation over centuries, discussing their roles in both acute and chronic consumption, and their potential impact on circadian rhythms. The study suggests potential clinical applications for improving sleep-related disorders and stresses the need for further research to understand the precise regulation and influence of these substances on sleep, particularly regarding circadian clock mechanisms.
  • Adenosine measurements in cortex.html -
    The research paper "GRABAdo1.0: A Novel Sensor for Extracellular Adenosine Detection and Its Role in Sleep Homeostasis" presents a study on glutamatergic neurons in the basal forebrain (BF), demonstrating their role as key regulators of sleep homeostasis through adenosine release. The study found higher adenosine levels during wakefulness and REM sleep compared to NREM sleep, and revealed a correlation between calcium activity in BF cholinergic neurons and adenosine levels. Optogenetic activation increased extracellular adenosine levels, while lesioning these glutamatergic neurons resulted in increased wakefulness during the active period, less recovery sleep after prolonged wakefulness, and impaired sleep rebound. The 'ion (38)' experiment showed statistically significant differences in NREM sleep time between the lesion group and control group. The discussion section reviews existing studies on sleep regulation and neural activity in the basal forebrain, confirming adenosine's role in mediating homeostatic control of sleep in cholinergic BF neurons, and contributing new insights into how neural activity during wakefulness contributes to sleep pressure through the release of sleep-inducing factors by BF glutamatergic neurons.

Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
  • GABAA receptor gating of proprioceptive afferent transmission--serotonin.html -
    The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.

engineering

  • ESS and autonomic dysreflexia.html -
    The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI. Key findings suggest vGLUT2ON excitatory neurons are activated during AD, with Vsx2ON being the most transcriptionally responsive subpopulation. The study also discusses an implantable system designed to restore hemodynamic stability in individuals with spinal cord injuries, featuring epidural electrodes, a brain-computer interface, and a closed-loop control system, which showed improved results compared to the control group in preclinical models and one human participant. The research highlights potential applications of this system beyond SCI management, but acknowledges limitations such as biocompatibility, long-term stability, and safety concerns for future optimization.
  • Inspired CO2 in hypoxia improves endurance.html -
    This double-blind pilot study investigates the potential benefits of low-dose CO2 inhalation for enhancing oxygenation and exercise capacity at high altitudes, suggesting it may alleviate symptoms of Acute Mountain Sickness (AMS) and preserve functional performance. However, further research is required to confirm these findings and explore long-term effects on exercise performance and health outcomes.
  • Kilgard VNS and SCI copy.html -
    The study demonstrates that Closed-Loop Vagus Nerve Stimulation (CLV) combined with rehabilitation significantly improves upper limb function recovery in individuals with chronic spinal cord injuries, showing a medium-sized effect size over sham stimulation, while being safe and effective. However, future studies are needed for confirmation of its efficacy.
  • V2a IPSC and cSCI Lana.html -
    The study by Zholudeva et al., published in 2026, found that engineered human spinal interneurons (specifically, V2a-enriched ones) demonstrated functional connections with injured rat spinal motor neurons when transplanted. Post-transplantation, the cells synaptically integrated with the injured phrenic motor network and maturated into multiple excitatory interneuron subtypes and glial populations within the host network. The study also identified genes specific to V2a spinal interneurons synapsing with the host phrenic motor network, such as SLITRK5 and PLXNA3, suggesting a promising approach for treating spinal cord injuries using endogenous human cells.
  • V2a neurons and SCI Lana Z.html -
    The study by Zholudeva et al. (2026) explores the feasibility of human V2a spinal interneurons derived from optogenetically engineered human induced pluripotent stem cells as a potential treatment for repairing spinal cord injuries in rats, demonstrating their ability to survive, integrate anatomically and synaptically connect with denervated host networks. However, further research is needed to optimize transplantation techniques, assess efficacy in larger animal models or clinical trials, and address limitations like ectopic migration, variability in cell survival, and the influence of the local injury environment on differentiation or maturation of transplanted cells.
  • e0315252026.full.html -
    The scientific review 'Magnetogenetics: Tools for Noninvasive Neuromodulation with Cellular Resolution' discusses a novel approach to control neuronal activity using magnetic nanoparticles or protein ferritin as transducers, addressing the limitations of chemogenetics and optogenetics. While promising in potential noninvasive, wireless, and depth-unrestricted methods for neuromodulation, ongoing challenges include understanding the underlying mechanisms, improving reproducibility, and developing standardized protocols.
  • neural mechanisms of spinal cord stim.html -
    In the study 'Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation' (Balaguer et al., 2025), researchers discovered that residual brain inputs modulate motoneuron membrane potentials, allowing subthreshold SCS pulses to transform into action potentials and entrain motoneuron activity to the stimulation. The study found that only a subset of SCS parameters enables volitional motor control, and this functional parameter space shrinks with lesion severity. The findings have potential implications for treating neurological disorders like spinal cord injuries, where SCS could help restore motor function by allowing participants to leverage residual supraspinal inhibition to control motoneuron firing rate. However, the study's limitations include focusing on immediate SCS effects and not accounting for long-term changes due to physical training.
  • neuroprostheses.html -
    The scientific paper presents a hybrid Dual Neuromodulation (DNB) system that combines an intracortical brain-computer interface, transcutaneous spinal cord stimulation, and peripheral stimuli for restoring motor and sensory functions in individuals with spinal cord injuries. The study on one participant showed significant improvements in motor control and sensory recovery, with potential for home use. Two related studies were discussed, both focusing on neural modulation in response to tactile stimuli and emphasizing the importance of understanding brain activity associated with different body parts and various types of stimuli. Challenges remain in optimizing stimulation parameters, adapting to interindividual variability, and streamlining identification of effective stimulation patterns for the DNB system.
  • s41596-026-01406-1.html -
    This protocol article describes a low-cost, standardized method for training head-fixed mice to self-administer drugs using 3D-printed equipment and open-source software (REACHER/Labrynth). The aim is to facilitate research on drug reward and motivation in rodents using head fixation systems. Key steps include habituating mice, training nose poke behavior, validating self-administration procedures, and implementing experimental manipulations like modulating drug doses or reward schedules. The method is compatible with neurotechnologies requiring mechanical stability for studying substance use disorders at unprecedented resolution.

general_neurobiology

  • 1-s2.0-S016622362600010X-main.html -
    This scientific paper explores the complex regulation of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF), a protein essential for brain development, learning, and memory, focusing on various transcription factors and four enhancer regions within the BDNF gene. The research also emphasizes post-transcriptional regulation of BDNF, underscoring the roles of distinct 5' and 3' UTRs in controlling subcellular localization and translation. The study highlights the need for further investigation into the regulatory landscape, non-neural tissue expression, and physiological significance of 3' UTRs in regulating cell type-specific BDNF expression to better understand its intricate regulatory processes and implications for brain health and disease.
  • 1-s2.0-S0960982226002459-main.html -
    In a study published in Current Biology, Yamada et al. identified wPBN Oxtr neurons as key regulators of water consumption and discrimination in animals. These neurons facilitate water intake and are crucial for water discrimination, as inhibition of these neurons impairs the ability of mice to distinguish water from other liquids. The study offers insights into potential targets for therapies related to disorders involving fluid intake, such as anorexia nervosa and Prader-Willi syndrome. However, further research is needed to clarify the specific subpopulation responsible for water perception and the molecular basis of water perception by identifying associated peripheral sensory end organs.
  • CNS Neuroscience Therapeutics - 2026 - Jing - The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates.html -

  • CNS lactate and neuroplasticity - 2026.html -
    The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.
  • GABAA receptor gating of proprioceptive afferent transmission--serotonin.html -
